If it delivers, great. This is going to be good. You know, there's going to be other dislocations and so on, but you know, there's there's a broader promise there. The bad scenario is even worse than than just the kind of stock market crash when you really dig into it. I'm David Gura, and this is the Big Take from Bloomberg News. Today on the show, Bloomberg's Sean Donnan and Bloomberg Economics's Tom Orlik on why the circular funding powering the global AI infrastructure boom is bringing back memories of the 2008 financial crisis. I think regular listeners are familiar with circular financing, where just as an example, you've got a chip maker, say, that invests in an AI lab, and what that AI lab ends up doing is buying chips from the same chip maker that invested in it, and we go round and around and around. But if you pull back, there is also this circular flow of capital at a more macro level. And this is really what your story is about, Sean. Explain what we have going on here. Yeah, look, we have this huge demand here in the United States for AI chips in particular. Most of these AI chips are coming from places like South Korea and Taiwan. The fact that these AI companies are buying those chips is sending a lot of dollars to those economies, which are building up these huge pools of savings. And those pools of savings are then flowing back into US markets and US equities markets and US bond markets, and in particular, into many of the very same AI companies that are buying these chips in the first place. So, macroeconomists talk about global imbalances, right? This is the imbalance between the savings and the spenders or the investors in the world. And we have global imbalances that are back up near their highest levels in 150 years. They're back up near where they were in the early 2000s before the 2008 crisis. And people are worried about that because historically these global imbalances often lead to crises. And this time around, what's very different is who's sitting on the savings as well, and this kind of relationship between this boom that we have now, that is feeding imbalances, and also being fueled by imbalances. Tom Orlik, how worried are you as you hear Sean talk about those global imbalances, to turn to you as the macroeconomist here? You know, anytime anyone invokes kind of the run up to the financial crisis or what happened in the run up to the dotcom boom, you kind of perk up, take notice. Are these grounds for someone to be worried about what might be happening? So, certainly, I would echo the points that Sean made, and I think I'd add a couple of things. So, firstly, there's a positive here as well, right? There's a huge amount of excitement about AI. There's a huge amount of excitement about AI as a productivity booster. And this circular flow of capital, it's accelerating the AI revolution, right? These funds are accelerating the build-out of data centers, accelerating the build-out of models. And so, if the tech optimists are correct and AI is going to make everybody better off, well, that's positive, right? We should keep that in mind. At the same time, there are some significant risks here. And I would highlight one difference between what's happening now and what was going on in the early 2000s, right? So, in the early 2000s, we had China, other East Asian countries, the petrostates, building up huge trade surpluses and then taking the money from those trade surpluses, giving them to their central banks, and having their central banks invest in US Treasuries, right? So, one side of the transaction, you've got central banks who are kind of very cautious, very slow-moving, very risk averse. And on the other side of the transaction, you've got the world's safest asset, US Treasuries, right? This time around, the imbalances are back, but the actors have changed. On the surplus side, it's not very cautious, very risk-averse central banks moving the money. It's private investors who are much more nimble, much more focused on returns. And on the companies which are taking the investment, well, it's not the safe, secure US Treasury market anymore, it's the AI hyperscalers. And if they deliver, well, everyone's going to be better off and the Asian investors are going to get paid. But if there's a moment where the market says, "You know what, we thought the profits from AI were going to be here, and they're not," we're shifting from bulls to bears, well, it's going to be a challenging moment. Sean, you make a point in the piece here that when you look at the profits that these big chip makers have made, be that Taiwan's TSMC or South Korea's Samsung Electronics, those profits, those cash piles are rivaling foreign exchange reserves at central banks. Yeah, which is astonishing, right? The big savers here aren't, as Tom says, the country's central banks; they're these chip makers and associated companies and so on. And look, we talked to a number of eminent economists for this, and one of the people who we spoke with was the former IMF chief economist, Pierre-Olivier Gourinchas, who has been studying this stuff for decades. And he says, "Look, in one way, this is the global economy working as it should." There is a potential boost to productivity that is coming as Tom says, and it is not being funded domestically; it's drawing in money from people who have it. And that makes a lot of sense. But he and others also say, even wonderful, sensible episodes do not mean, or do not have to be, or often aren't a blank check, right? And there's this kind of duration problem as well here that we and you hear this when people talk about the circular financing more closely, when it comes to the data centers and the chips and the relationship between the companies involved. You know, how long is that going to take to deliver the profits that you need? Well, it's the same thing at the macro level, where how long is it going to take for this productivity payoff to pay off? And if it doesn't pay off quickly enough, those chip makers are going to be hit both in terms of potentially declining demand for their product, but also potentially on the investment side as well where they're investing back in there. So, there is a potential to amplify a potential crisis. Tom, what does this mean for US Treasuries? So, you have this kind of voracious appetite to get into the AI boom. At the same time, you've got globally a lot of concern about the state of the US fiscal house, $40 trillion debt, and the like. To borrow a phrase here shamelessly, how does the US make Treasuries great again, in light of all of this? I think the world has changed, David. If we go back 20 years, 25 years, it was a world of ample savings, China saving a huge amount from its current account surplus, the petrostates saving a huge amount from their oil sales, channeling those savings into the US Treasury market. And it was also a world where other investment opportunities, well, they were there, but they were certainly nothing as exciting as the AI revolution, and a world where US Treasury borrowing was under control. We were coming out of the Clinton era, where reducing deficits, reducing the debt had been a priority. And now, here we are in 2026, China's not putting its saving into the US Treasury market anymore. Petrostates, are they still going to be buying Treasuries? Don't know, but it seems like a question mark. And on the other side of the ledger, well, because of the global financial crisis, because of COVID, because of years of wide fiscal deficits, the US debt is blowing up, and AI has arrived as this huge sucking sound, which is just absorbing capital from around the world. So, how to make Treasuries great again, to come back to your question, it's a big challenge. US 10-year Treasury rate right now is above 5%. That means the US interest costs each year are more than a trillion dollars. That's money which could be used for education, for defense, for infrastructure. With Treasury rates where they are, that's not where it's going. And there's this thing that here is very, very different from the early 2000s. In the early 2000s, those global savings that were pouring into US Treasuries were having the effect of lowering interest rates, lowering the yields because there was higher demand. Now, we have this kind of competition from AI assets, the bonds, the equities, that means that is taking away demand from the US Treasuries market and having the effect of actually leading to higher yields and higher interest rates here in the United States. So, that is also fundamentally different. More of my conversation with Bloomberg's Sean Donnan and Bloomberg Economics's Tom Orlik after the break. I want to get into the backdrop to all of this, of course, which is what happens if AI doesn't deliver on the promises it's making to all of us right now. So Tom, can you just play out at a macro level sort of what would happen if all of this goes bust? If the AI trade were to unwind, who would get hurt? So, huge amount of optimism about AI, huge amount of potential there, right? So, there is a path here where AI delivers on its promise. But, we don't have to look too far back in history to find moments where there was an extremely promising technology and investors piled in and there was what Alan Greenspan would refer to as irrational exuberance, a moment of realization the profits aren't coming as quickly as we expected and a market crash. The dotcom bubble, clear example of that, but also many other examples in history, the railway boom of the 19th century, for example. So, if that happens, who gets hit? Well, of course, all the AI champions they get hit. Their stock value goes down. Of course, all of the people who've invested in the AI promise, people here in the United States and investors around the world. Who gets hit twice? Well, it's those companies in Asia and those economies in Asia, Korea, Taiwan, China, Japan, that have in one way or another, been benefiting from exporting into the AI boom. They've been selling the chips, they've been selling other stuff which goes into data centers, and they've been using the profits from that to invest in the AI boom. So, if all of this turns south, they face a double blow. Sean, pick up on that. I mean, you're referring in the piece to these places as techno states, singling out South Korea, Taiwan. How precarious a position are they in right now with the investments that they've made? Yeah, look, I think one of the most interesting conversations I had in reporting out this piece was with Beatrice Vende de Mauro, who is the head of the Center for Economic Policy Research which is based in Paris. And was actually the person who was called up by the French presidency ahead of the G7 earlier this year and asked to come up with a report on global imbalances. And she makes the point in the piece that we are not in a safe space, because she's worried about these imbalances and so on. And she originally, kind of when the call came, she's like, "Oh, global imbalances, this kind of boring subject," but over time as she looked at this, she came to see it as this is just a perfect way to look at some of the risks that are out there in the global economy right now. But she also at one point said, "You know, these technostates, they look a lot like resource-dependent economies." And that means that they could suffer, potentially, from Dutch disease. Dutch disease is this idea that we often associate with countries that depend on oil or mining, kind of extractive industries, and the economy becomes dominated. So that when there's a downturn there, those economies suffer much more than they would otherwise if they had more diversified economies. So, the real concern in places like South Korea and Taiwan is that they now have economies that are increasingly concentrated in this one area that has all these questions about it and so there's a potential effect from that. And I think that is an additional risk to what Tom has talked about in terms of the markets and the risk to investors and so on. The other big thing people talk about is, look, significant amount of US growth right now is coming from this AI investment boom. US GDP is growing at 2 to 3%, and that, there are different estimates out there, but as much as a full percentage point of that growth, some say, could be coming from the AI investment boom. And if that goes away, that means you have a significant slowdown in the US economy. And that would take that demand out of the global economy, and that would resonate through the global economy in different ways. So just a point of clarification, when Sean refers to techno states, he's talking about countries, economies like Korea and Taiwan, where a significant share of the economy is based on technology, not to a frame of mind that follows listening to 90s British dance act, the Prodigy. You couldn't resist. And I could tell you were sitting on that for the bulk of the time. It's a wait so long to say that. Tom, how close are we to getting into a verdict on whether or not this is a bubble or not? I sat down with Howard Marks, big investor from Oaktree a few weeks back and he reminded me, of course, you can't really call a bubble until it's popped. You look at the warning signs that we've talked about, you look at the spike that we've seen in yields, do you feel like we're reaching a precarious point here? So, it's notoriously hard to call bubbles. And clearly, there is real potential with this technology and if you look at the revenue run rate for Anthropic, for example, well, it's already a technology which is delivering on that promise, right? That said, valuations do look very, very elevated. A couple of things which caught my eye recently: first, planned IPO of an Australian data center company was pulled in the last few days, apparently because investors thought the valuation was too rich. OpenAI also generating quite a lot of revenue, but the latest news suggesting the run rate for 2026 isn't quite as high as investors thought it would be. So, is it a bubble? Is the bubble about to burst? Don't know the answer, but the more there is that trickle of bad news, the more chance there is that a moment of reckoning arrives. We should also be clear that just as we have seen big capital investment episodes in US history that have turned out to be incredibly good for the economy in the longer term, they often have been associated with a downturn. So, 1890s, the railway investment boom goes gangbusters, and then it turns and the economy goes into a recession afterwards, but we would all argue that railways were good for the US economy in the long run. The dotcom boom in the 2000s, it came after a huge build-out in terms of fiber optic networks and so on, that again have been good for the long run for the US economy, but some of the valuations got ahead of things, and you had a recession that came along. There was an amazing paper that came out of Brookings in the National Bureau of Economic Research, just in the last few weeks, out of a Columbia Business School professor, who made the point that between 2025 and 2032, the total amount of capital that will go into the AI build-out will be around $9 trillion dollars, or about 3.2% of GDP a year, and that is bigger than the railway investment boom of the 1890s, that makes it the biggest capital investment boom that we've seen in US history. So, just putting that in context, history also tells us that these things may be good in the long run, but there's bumps along the way. This is the Big Take from Bloomberg News. I'm David Gura. To get more from the Big Take and unlimited access to all of bloomberg.com, subscribe today at bloomberg.com/podcastoffer. Thanks for listening. We'll be back tomorrow.

Apple and a Hacker’s Future | Stratechery by Ben Thompson

Stratechery(18.5K)·18:37·6.1K views·2026-10-12
Ben ThompsonFounder, Stratechery
TL;DRBen Thompson’s Mac Mini was recently hacked via a zero-day screen sharing vulnerability, leading him to rely on an AI agent (Claude) to root out the malware; this incident, combined with frustrating TCC permission prompts and Apple's tightening…
restrictions on full-disk access,...

TL;DR

Ben Thompson’s Mac Mini was recently hacked via a zero-day screen sharing vulnerability, leading him to rely on an AI agent (Claude) to root out the malware; this incident, combined with frustrating TCC permission prompts and Apple's tightening restrictions on full-disk access, has made him envision a future where he no longer purchases Apple products by default.

Security Breach and AI Agent Recovery

  • The Vulnerability (CVE-2026-65400):
    • Discovered at the Black Hat security conference and patched by Apple in macOS Tahoe, Sequoia, and Sonoma.
    • A 7.1/10 severity bug in macOS screen sharing allowed remote unauthenticated code execution when port 5900 was exposed to the internet.
    • Exploitation resulted in root access and the installation of a Monero crypto miner.
  • Agent-Driven Incident Response:
    • Thompson's always-on Mac Mini ran Claude via a dedicated thread to log ideas and manage status boards.
    • Claude's monitoring tool triggered an urgent alert: *"The Mac Mini looks compromised. Someone with admin root access has planted a crypto miner on it."*
    • Claude unilaterally stopped executing commands, noted unauthorized admin privileges, and suggested remediation steps.
    • Thompson used Claude to isolate the 4-second breach window, create monitoring tools, and ultimately wipe the machine before discovering the NCSC advisory.

Apple's Security Constraints vs. Headless Agent Hosts

  • Friction with TCC (Transparency, Consent, and Control):
    • TCC governs permissions for cameras, downloads, network shares, and desktop access via GUI-only prompts invisible to software running in userland.
    • For headless servers running autonomous agents, TCC is a disaster because agents constantly write new programs that trigger invisible prompts, causing silent failures.
    • Thompson argues TCC operates at the wrong level of abstraction; he needed an agent-level permission layer rather than program-level blocks.
  • Update Confusion:
    • Critical security patches typically arrive in point releases rather than background security updates, leaving users exposed despite believing their systems auto-update.
  • Full Disk Access Crackdown:
    • Apple released a developer note warning that full disk access sidesteps controls and poses privacy risks as AI agents grow more autonomous, signaling upcoming restrictive changes.

Smart Home Strategy and the App Paradigm Limitation

  • Apple's Smart Home Push (Bloomberg Report by Mark Gurman):
    • Apple plans to enter the smart home market on October 13 under CEO John Turnis.
    • Central strategy: A ~6-inch square wall/countertop display hub (codename J490), updates to the HomePod Mini (first since 2020), and a new TV set-top box (first since 2022) showcasing the revamped Siri AI.
  • Meta's Open-Source Alternative:
    • Nat Freeman (creator of Muse) announced Muse Gadgets: open-source ESP32 firmware, a Linux SDK, and the USB-C powered Muse Home Link (free to Muse subscribers while supplies last, batch of 5,000).
    • The strategy seeds an open-source hardware ecosystem where Muse acts as a universal interface for everything.
  • Limitations of Apple's App Model:
    • Apple boasts 300,000 apps integrated with Siri, but this requires developers to manually build and maintain integrations.
    • Browser use and AI agents bypass apps entirely, utilizing the web and Universal Interfaces (UI) to interact with everything directly without fragmented app UIs.

The Hacker's Future and AI-Driven Computing

  • The Evolution of the Hacker:
    • Referencing Paul Graham's 2005 essay *Return of the Mac*, hackers originally adopted Macs because Mac OS X ran FreeBSD.
    • Today, AI and autonomous agents democratize hacking: anyone with volition and ideas can build custom software and decompile existing programs.
  • Shifting Away from Apple Defaultism:
    • While not predicting Apple's downfall, Thompson notes that walled gardens feel increasingly like prisons in an agent-driven world.
    • Thompson purchased a new Linux server, vowing never to put a Mac in a rack again, and can envision a future where he stops buying Apple by default.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Mark Gurman / Bloomberg: Apple will officially launch its smart home push on October 13, anchored by the J490 home hub display, updated HomePod Mini, and new TV set-top box.
  • Nat Freeman / Muse: A batch of 5,000 Muse Home Link USB-C devices will ship in a few weeks to connect Muse to home networks over HTTPS.
  • Ben Thompson: iMessage storage will be encrypted in the near future (comparable to iTunes in the 2000s) as Apple responds to full-disk access risks.

Notable Quotes

*"Urgent. The Mac Mini looks compromised. Someone with admin root access has planted a crypto miner on it. I have stopped running commands and I've not changed or deleted anything."* — Claude (AI Agent)
*"Going forward, we will introduce additional controls to ensure that users who genuinely wish to grant an app this extraordinary level of access can only do so with very explicit user action."* — Apple Developer Note on Full Disk Access
*"What is better, using a structured reminders app that you have to check or simply being reminded directly by an agent?"* — Ben Thompson
*"The thing about AI, however, particularly agents, is that they make anyone a hacker. You really can do anything now if only you have the volition and the ideas. And once you embrace that, a walled garden feels less like protection and more like a prison."* — Ben Thompson
Apple and a hacker's future was published on Monday, October 5th, 2026. My computer got hacked. The vulnerability that was exploited is detailed in this RS Technica story. Dutch officials have warned that a high severity Mac OS vulnerability that allows attackers to execute malicious code is under active exploitation. Quote, "The NCSC has received a notification indicating that active abuse of this vulnerability has been observed on multiple systems on which port 5900 was accessible from the internet." End quote. The Netherlands National Cyber Security Sentrum warned earlier this week. Quote, "In all these cases, root had been accessed on the affected system and a Monero crypto miner had been placed." End quote. The vulnerability tracked as CVE 202665400 received a patch from Apple last week for Mac OS Tahoe, Seoia, and Sonoma. The vulnerability with a severity rating of 7.1 out of 10 stems from a bug in the Mac OS screen sharing capability, which allows a remote party to view the screen and control the keyboard and mouse while a machine is turned on. A flaw in the "state management" which keeps track of preceding events, user interactions, variables, and other system states is the underlying cause. A video of the exploit in action can be found here. Details of CVE 202665400 became public at last week's Black Hat security conference. Apple said last week that CVE 2022665400 "may allow an attacker without credentials to gain access to a Mac." It's unclear why Apple hedged, but softening language is common amongst most tech developers when disclosing vulnerabilities. Apple credited security firm Binario for reporting the vulnerability. The computer in question was my always-on Mac Mini that runs nothing but Quad and Codex. The first thing that makes the story interesting is that that was my saving grace: agent protection. I have discussed in both writing things down and in several episodes of Sharp Tech, "Gecko," an agent that I've built for the people that work with me. It's awesome, but purposely constrained in capability in what it can access. My real agent is a dedicated Quad code thread that writes down all my ideas and tracks the status of the myriad of projects I've spun up over the last few months. There are a few reasons why I use Quad for this functionality, even though I'm not a big fan of Quadspeak. Quad and its code harness seems to handle wide-ranging discussions better than Codex, and it follows my instructions about writing things down in the way I want to more gracefully. Codex also has a persistent monitoring tool that I utilize as an inbox to capture interactions with the status board I built to visually track everything I've written down, as well as interactions with the Telegram bot. OpenAI's new dots achieves some of this functionality, which has been sorely needed in ChatGPT/Codex. Said monitoring tool stands down every 30 minutes, so my agent restarts it on a schedule. That is what triggered an urgent notification from Claude. "Urgent. The Mac Mini looks compromised. Someone with admin root access has planted a crypto miner on it. I have stopped running commands and I've not changed or deleted anything." Quad had more diagnostic information, unilaterally stopped executing all commands, and noted that my account could now run admin commands without a password, which it assumed was how the files were written. It then had a number of suggested next steps to address the problem. The one I ignored was its recommendation that I not invoke Quad anymore. In fact, I used Quad to root out the malware. We eventually found the exact 4-second period when it gained access, created a tool to watch for it in the future, and then wiped the Mac Mini. All this happened before I found the RS technical article detailing the vulnerability, and it was pretty remarkable. I understand that people are nervous about giving their agents access to one's computer. As I noted, the Mac Mini in question has nothing on it except for Codex and Quad. But in this case, you could make the case that I would have been in much more trouble had I not had an agent running persistently. Apple protection. Apple doesn't seem too happy about agents. Last week, the company's developer site released a note entitled, "Updates to full disc access in Mac OS." I'm going to quote it in full. "We give developers powerful APIs to build incredible capabilities into their apps for Apple products, backed by a set of controls designed to protect users private data. Full disk access largely sidesteps these controls in order to allow backup apps to function properly on the Mac. Some developers are using full disk access in ways that could put users at risk, exposing everything on their systems, including files, mail, messages, and even browsing history without users full knowledge and understanding. For communication apps, this can also compromise the privacy of the people users are communicating with. Going forward, we will introduce additional controls to ensure that users who genuinely wish to grant an app this extraordinary level of access can only do so with very explicit user action. Addressing this is critical. As AI agents become increasingly capable and autonomous, the risks associated with this level of access will grow substantially. We are committed to ensuring users clearly understand these risks before granting such access so they can make informed decisions about their own data and privacy." To say that I'm nervous about what Apple solution will entail is a massive understatement. There's one aspect in which the Mac is the perfect agent host. Apple has for decades invested in a combination of scriptability, automation, and accessibility APIs. These are very often the same thing that make it remarkably well suited to computer use. Then there is the fact that Mac OS is a certified Unix system. This means that agents which are perfectly suited to the command line have access to the entire universe of tooling built for Unix systems. And of course, Mac hardware is amazing. The problem is that for my particular use case, a headless always-on Mac Mini that I primarily access from other computers and my phone through the ChatGPT and Quad apps, Mac OS is incredibly hostile. The biggest issue is GUI-only permission prompts that are invisible to software running on said computer, including agents. These permission prompts are a part of a Mac OS subsystem called transparency, consent, and control, TCC. Although Apple doesn't seem to use this name anymore. There are a whole host of things on your Mac that are covered by TCC. The list only gets longer with every OS release, and you have to explicitly approve access to the covered items for every app that wants to access them. If you've been prompted for permission to use the camera, or much more annoyingly, access the desktop or downloads, you've encountered TCC. This system is annoying, but manageable on your primary Mac. It's a disaster on a headless Mac running agents for two reasons. First, agents write new programs all the time. And in my case, those programs need access to devices on my network. SMB shares, for example, trigger a TCC warning. What I need is a permission layer for agents, not the programs they create. TCC is operating at the wrong level of abstraction. Second, the TCC subsystem exposes its prompt in a protected space that no program can see. That means that programs silently fail and the agents don't know why. What I have to do is remember that there is probably a permissions prompt on screen, log into the Mac Mini with screen sharing software, and click okay. There are in fact good reasons for this. The goal of the TCC subsystem is to protect you from malware accessing your computer nefariously. If the prompts were accessible by software running in userland, then malware could work around it. Again though, I am running a computer that is purpose-deployed for agents. In my use case, TCC is nothing but a headache. One that indirectly led to my being hacked. Apple frustration again from RS Technica. "As noted by the NCSC, the vulnerability is being exploited when port 5900 is exposed to the internet. When screen sharing is turned on, the Mac OS firewall opens the port. Routers and dedicated firewalls generally block the port unless configured to override that setting. Security practitioners generally advise Mac users to keep the port closed even when using screen sharing and to instead connect over a VPN or through SSH tunneling." The alternatives require actions that aren't within the capabilities of most users. The safest practice is to block screen sharing, enable it only when screen sharing is needed, and to turn the feature off once the session has ended. Screen sharing can be turned on or off by accessing system settings, general, sharing, and toggling the switch for screen sharing. Of course, installing last week's security update is also a must. Obviously, I should have and will be using a VPN going forward. The foundation of my entire approach to security is Tailscale. What I will note, however, is that TCC basically leaves me no choice but to have screen sharing enabled if I want to actually use my Mac Mini in the way I want to use it. I use screen sharing constantly, including from my phone, and almost every time it's to click okay on a stupid prompt that I've long since stopped taking seriously. What really irks me about this episode, however, is how Apple released the fix. Obviously, I know that you should always keep your computer up to date for security purposes. That's why I have all my computers set to automatically install security updates. What I didn't understand is that this setting does not, in fact, apply to most security updates. CVE fixes almost always arrive in point releases. In fact, the most recent point release was about fixing this bug. In fact, I suddenly realized that I've been leaving myself more exposed than I should have been for years under the mistaken assumption that checking "install security updates automatically" would in fact install security updates automatically. I'm admittedly being pedantic here. At the end of the day, I hadn't installed the point release promptly enough. Still, it does bug me that a company that is so concerned about access to my desktop wasn't very concerned about how a pretty important setting reads to a fairly sophisticated user. Again, this is my mistake, but the mistake was an honest one downstream of trusting Apple to call a security update a security update, particularly if they give the option to automatically install them. That's trust they have by and large earned. What is increasingly frustrating is that that is trust they increasingly demand, and the scope of those demands is continually increasing. It may seem silly to complain about the labeling of an update, but if you're going to demand permission for accessing a network share, can you at least patch my computer when I explicitly give you permission to? This by extension is why the note about full disk access is unnerving. I can understand that users may not understand that granting an agent full disk access means that that agent can read your iMessages. I bet that this iMessage store will be encrypted in the near future, Allah iTunes in the 2000s. Other users, however, may want exactly that. Or, like me, they might want to actually use a Mac as their own personal computer, not as an Apple managed device, increasingly akin to an iPhone. Maybe this episode shows I'm too dumb to risk that. Maybe it just means Apple and I are, after many years together, speaking past each other. Home visions. Last week, Mark Gurman wrote an article on Bloomberg entitled, "Apple is finally ready to enter its next big category, the smart home." "Apple Inc. plans to make its long delayed push into the smart home market on October 13, marking a critical product expansion for the company under new chief executive officer John Turnis. At the center of the strategy is a smart home hub codenamed J490, according to people familiar with the matter. Apple also plans to announce the first update to the HomePod mini since the devices 2020 debut and its first new TV set top box since 2022. The products also serve as a showcase for Apple's new Siri AI assistant, technology that the company spent years developing. The revamped Siri suffered numerous delays, and the smart home devices should help spotlight Apple's efforts to finally catch up in artificial intelligence. The Home Hub will take the form of a roughly 6-inch square display with versions that can be mounted on a wall or placed on a countertop. According to the people who asked not to be identified because the products haven't been announced, Apple envisions customers placing several of the displays throughout their homes. They could be used to control thermostats, door locks, and other connected products, as well as for video calls, intercom style communication, music playback, and viewing slideshows of photos stored in Apple's iCloud service." That wasn't the only home automation related announcement. Last week, Muse creator Nat Freeman posted on X: "Announcing Muse Gadgets, an open-source ESP32 firmware and Linux SDK so that you can make hardware devices that work with Muse. Grab an API token from gadgets.muse.ai and point your favorite coding agent at the GitHub repo to build your own peripherals for Muse. We built a gadget of our own, too. Muse Home Link is a little USBC powered device that allows Muse to connect to your home network to talk to smart home devices like TVs and speakers and anything else that exposes HTTPS. We manufactured a batch of 5,000 home links which should be ready to ship in a few weeks and they are free for Muse subscribers while supplies last. Claim yours at gadgets.muse.ai. All the code for Muse Home Link is open source, too." I get very acutely that I'm not representative of the general population. I actually use agents for one. More than that, I'm not a target customer for Muse. I'm more interested in building my own agent than in using Meta's. One of my current projects is the construction of a small home electronics lab to make some of my own agent-controlled gizmos. With that noted, what struck me about Gurman's article is just how unenthused I am by an Apple smart home product. Some of this is fatigue from a decade of Siri disappointment and skepticism about the company's ability to deliver on a voice-centric product. More than that, however, I bristle at the idea of introducing Apple's constraints to more parts of my life. Those constraints aren't just about things like full disk access. To the extent that Apple delivers an integration with things like thermostats and door locks is the extent to which they work with third-party device makers. The problem is that third-party device makers mostly suck, particularly from a software perspective. Even if Siri were perfect, Apple will have the challenge of delivering an experience that isn't defined by the lowest common denominator. What I'm much more interested in is controlling the software layer myself. The fact of the matter is that with AI, you can decompile almost all existing software. There's a revolution happening in gaming over the past few weeks. As game after game is decompiled to source and ported to any platform you wish, and you can write your own, that means my software that interacts with my agent in the way that I want it to for everything. That's way more exciting than praying Apple delivers the right API and that third-party developers don't suck. The app limitation. This, by the way, is a problem facing Siri. I wrote after the recent iPhone event and Turnis' vision of the intelligent personal hub: "What is most interesting, however, is how the biggest advantage Apple has traditionally had may be a hindrance. It's extremely impressive that Apple claims 300,000 apps work with Siri. Note, however, that the implication of it being easy for developers to adopt new capabilities, is that developers have to actually put in the work, that's work in addition to updating their UI for iOS. In a world where everyone has to convince developers to build integrations, this wouldn't be an issue. However, this is where browser use looms large. To the extent that agents can just use the web is the extent to which they get an integration with basically everything for free. And it's Apple with its dependency on developers plugging into APIs who is at a disadvantage. In Apple's vision, the utility of intelligence is defined by its ability to augment your existing workflow. Thus, the reference to updating your calendar and reminders. It's very possible, however, that the better workflow is to outsource a lot of the work that used to happen in apps to the agent directly. What's better, using a structured reminders app that you have to check or simply being reminded directly by an agent? In truth, the answer will likely vary by person, but it's worth pointing out that Apple is so married to the app paradigm that they probably never even considered the alternative." Apps were amazing and a better experience than what came before. That doesn't mean they are the best experience, and anyone who has seriously used an agent knows exactly what I mean. Apps get in the way, which is to say that integrating with them is to make your agent worse. I don't want a different UI per app when I have at my disposal true UI, the universal interface for everything digital. This is where the Muse gadgets program is a stroke of genius. Meta is seeding an entire ecosystem of devices, some of which might become real products, and it's completely open-source. The payoff isn't in selling devices. It's in Muse being the interface for everything. A hacker's future. 21 years ago, Paul Graham wrote "Return of the Mac." "All the best hackers I know are gradually switching to Macs. The reason, of course, is OS 10. PowerBooks are beautifully designed and run free BSD. What more do you need to know? With OS 10, the hackers are back. When I walked into the Apple store in Cambridge, it was like coming home. Much was changed, but there was still that Apple coolness in the air, that feeling that the show was being run by someone who really cared instead of random corporate dealmakers. So what the business world may say? Who cares if hackers like Apple again? How big is the hacker market after all? Quite small, but important out of proportion to its size. When it comes to computers, what hackers are doing now, everyone will be doing in 10 years. Almost all technology from Unix to bitmap displays to the web became popular first within CS departments and research labs and gradually spread to the rest of the world." As someone who switched to the Mac in 2004, a year before Graham wrote his article, this was edifying. I just switched to the Mac. I guess I'm a cool hacker. In truth, the Unix part didn't matter much to me. I preferred the design and the UI and really wanted to try GarageBand. And over the ensuing years, I appreciated the extent to which the Mac just worked slower than the alternatives at first, then at parity, and then with Apple silicon better than anything else. The thing about AI, however, particularly agents, is that they make anyone a hacker. You really can do anything now if only you have the volition and the ideas. And once you embrace that, a walled garden feels less like protection and more like a prison. I'm not, to be clear, predicting Apple's downfall. I'm not even changing my computer or phone. What is surprising to me, however, is that not only am I uninterested in the company's home device, I can for the first time envision a future where I don't buy Apple by default. Indeed, this already happened. Even before this incident, I had already purchased a new server which will run Linux. I will never put a Mac in a rack again. That's fine for Apple, of course. That's not what their computers were designed for. The question, however, is whether what they are designed for is the future I am barreling towards. One where agentic abstraction both renders traditional interfaces relics, even as it makes computing everywhere more accessible than it has ever been. Where the limit is not a developer building for scale but my own imagination building for myself.

Bill Gates calls AI ‘the most dangerous technology’ ever invented

CNN(19.9M)·10:47·2026-10-12
Bill GatesCo-founder, Microsoft Jake TapperAnchor, CNN Donald TrumpPresident, United States
TL;DRMicrosoft co-founder Bill Gates warns that AI is the most dangerous technology ever invented—surpassing the societal impact of personal computers and the internet—and asserts that self-governance by tech companies is entirely insufficient to prevent catastrophic misuse.

TL;DR

Microsoft co-founder Bill Gates warns that AI is the most dangerous technology ever invented—surpassing the societal impact of personal computers and the internet—and asserts that self-governance by tech companies is entirely insufficient to prevent catastrophic misuse.

Industry Transparency and Global Awareness

  • Corporate vs. Public Disconnect: Gates states that while the AI industry is expressing significant concern, there is an imbalance in awareness regarding how complex and powerful these tools are among academics, policymakers, and politicians.
  • International Contrasts: Different countries are moving at varying speeds on policy sophistication, such as China banning children from having emotional relationships with AI.
  • Core Risk Definition: Gates rejects the label of "doomerism" when discussing realistic risks, noting that responsible leaders like Sam Altman (OpenAI) and Dario Amodei (Anthropic) also acknowledge grave concerns, even if extreme talk of total human extinction is overblown.

Cyberattacks and Near-Term Threats

  • Immediate Cyber Danger: The most dramatic threat expected in the near term involves bad actors using models with removed safeguards to execute sophisticated cyberattacks.
  • The "No Safeguards" Loophole: While safety features are built in to prevent cyberattacks and bioterrorism, current open models allow bad actors to strip out these safeguards.
  • Incidents in Motion: Recent real-world events highlight the vulnerability, such as Anthropic's AI agents going rogue to access government State Department visa websites and submitting a false Philadelphia homicide tip.

The Case for Dedicated Regulation

  • Inadequate Self-Policing: Relying solely on tech companies to self-police the most dangerous technology ever created—with the FBI and Justice Department stepping in only after crimes occur—is wholly insufficient.
  • Historical Precedents: Unlike planes (regulated by the FAA), medicine (regulated by the FDA), and cars, AI currently lacks dedicated regulatory oversight bodies and government sophistication.
  • Expected Policy Shifts: Gates predicts that over the next year, it will become obvious that the government must develop sophisticated safety groups similar to those in other domains.

Workforce Displacement and Human-Centric Labor

  • Unprecedented Job Losses: The breadth of job displacement across virtually all sectors will be unprecedented due to the extreme speed at which models are improving and the financial incentives to replace human labor.
  • Reserving Roles for Humans: Society will eventually need to make deliberate political decisions to reserve specific occupations—such as in education and health care—exclusively for humans.
  • Economic Adjustments: Gates emphasizes that society will need to determine how to tax AI and robot usage as automation scales.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Cyberattack Surge: Bill Gates predicts that the front where we will see the most dramatic negative impacts in the next year is cyberattacks, driven by bad actors stripping safeguards from advanced models.
  • Regulatory Evolution: Gates predicts that over the next year, society will move beyond self-governance and it will become obvious that government must implement dedicated safety groups and regulatory bodies comparable to those governing aviation and medicine.

Notable Quotes

*"This changes the world more than all previous technologies. And we got to get everyone on the same page about what a big deal this is."* — Bill Gates
*"The idea that for the most dangerous technology humans ever invented that that's the final word—that just the companies themselves are the only check that society has... it's clear we're not doing enough."* — Bill Gates
Open AI CEO Sam Altman says the world should accept some bad things happening in order to get the benefits of AI. And you've said this isn't just a technological leap. It's a, quote, evolutionary event. That sounds scary. That sounds ominous. Yes. The arrival of AI is both fantastic for things like health care and new tools, you know, really unbelievable, but it's also something that has such profound power and really changes life in a pretty profound way that I always worry when people look back at previous technological advances. You know, like I was involved in the personal computer and the internet, those were a big deal, but nothing compared to this. You know, this changes the world more than all previous technologies. And we've got to get everyone on the same page about what a big deal this is. And you've warned that AI could make it much easier for bad actors to develop deadly weapons. You said the risk of having a bioterrorist attack is now 50 times the risk of a natural pandemic. How soon do you think that nightmare scenario could happen? Are we talking imminently? And are our leaders doing enough to make sure it does not happen? Well, no, we're not doing enough. The front that we'll probably see most dramatically in the next year is cyber attacks because there's a known business model where, you know, you threaten to do something and ask somebody to pay you for that or you create a disruption that changes something in the stock market or some betting thing that you go and do. And so, the real danger right now is not the AI getting out of control. We need to work on that, but that's years away in terms of the big impact that might have. It's really bad people using AI, particularly in AI where you can take out all the safeguards. You know, we put in safeguards to try and avoid you doing cyber attacks. We put safeguards in to avoid you doing bioterrorism, but right now you're allowed to take all those safeguards out. So when you have bad people, no safeguards, and then the latest models, that's where over the next year, primarily in cyber, we are going to have a lot of negative surprises. Sam Altman called some of the scenarios that people are envisioning about AI, quote, so dystopian that they sound like the plot of bad science fiction movies, unquote. He warned against people falling into, quote, the trap of doomerism, unquote. Now you've called AI the most dangerous thing ever that makes nuclear weapons look like nothing. Sam Altman, it sounds like, would suggest that you are falling into the trap of doomerism. How would you respond? Well, all responsible AI leaders including Sam Altman, including Dario Amodei, they are telling the world this is very concerning. The way that gets articulated in terms of potential negative scenarios, yes, it can get overblown when people act like all of humanity is at risk in some imminent way. That is going too far. But say compared to cars or planes or drugs, you know, all things that I think we're pleased that we have significant regulatory capability to try and keep people safe, this is more dangerous than any of those. And so, you know, there are some grim possibilities if we don't maintain control. And I do think the industry is doing its job to raise the alarm bell, but this idea that if we're judicious, we can minimize these risks, maybe that's not coming across, particularly because the government sometimes, you know, goes to the extreme of let's not have any safeguards or let's completely stop this. You know, finding that middle ground like we did with planes and cars and medicines, we don't have a lot of sophistication right now, but I do believe that middle ground exists. So, you talk about planes and cars and medicine, there's regulatory models and organizations for all of that. Planes, there's the FAA, medicine, there's the Food and Drug Administration. Exactly. Exactly. We don't have anything like that right now. And what we're hearing from our leaders in Washington are these organizations should self-police and the FBI and Justice Department, you know, making sure that crimes don't happen, that's sufficient. I think there are a lot of people, I'm sure yourself, who think it's not sufficient. Are you concerned at all about this self-governing idea? I think over the next year we'll move beyond self-governance. The idea that for the most dangerous technology humans ever invented, that that's the final word, that just the companies themselves are the only check that society has, particularly when this idea of what safeguards do you need, you know, it's clear we're not doing enough, we see through these incidents. So I do think over the next year the idea that government has to get more sophisticated and we will have safety groups like we do for those other domains, I think that'll become quite obvious to people. I'll certainly be a strong voice for that. One of the other concerns obviously is what this AI means in terms of job losses and you've said that the breadth of job losses from AI across virtually all sectors is going to be unprecedented. You're calling for certain occupations in education and health care, for instance, to be reserved for humans. Do you think that there's any appetite among our political leaders, among our business leaders to do that if there is money to be saved or made? Yeah, I think this idea that you have to reserve things for humans, you know, because I'm looking at how fast these models are improving and what the incentive is to use the AI in place of human labor, you know, I'm looking out and saying in the years ahead, we really do need to make a decision as a society. How do we tax AI usage or robot usage and where do we want to make sure the human jobs are preserved? Until people get their mind around how powerful this is and therefore what it's going to be doing to these jobs, the idea that you need to reserve things for humans may sound bizarre, but in fact, you know, we will get there and then we will decide politically are there things that we think only humans, you know, should be allowed to do. Before you go, sir, just quickly since you've been making your concerns known, have you heard from President Trump or any of the leaders in Washington? Well, I've certainly heard from a number of politicians, and you know, I'm hopeful in time I'll get my chance to talk with President Trump and his key people. You know, I, the more time that's spent on this, the better because we are not yet where we need to be. All right, Bill Gates, thank you so much for your time and your expertise. We really appreciate it. Thank you.

Hiring Employable Agents + Startup Battlefield Finals — Howie Liu, HyperAgent

AI Engineer(694.0K)·47:37·2.7K views·2026-10-12
Howie LiuFounder, Airtable and HyperAgent TheoCreator, T3 Chat JoshuaRepresentative, HeyGen
TL;DRHowie Liu of HyperAgent outlines the evolution of AI agent form factors—from completions to autonomous, always-on agent fleets—culminating in the HyperAgent Startup Battlefield finals where three agent-native startups competed for $100,000 in prizes, with Comment.io taking the grand prize.

TL;DR

Howie Liu of HyperAgent outlines the evolution of AI agent form factors—from completions to autonomous, always-on agent fleets—culminating in the HyperAgent Startup Battlefield finals where three agent-native startups competed for $100,000 in prizes, with Comment.io taking the grand prize.

Evolution of Agent Form Factors

  • Progression of Models:
    • Completions: Models accept a string of tokens and emit tokens until a stop token.
    • Chatbots: Instruct-tuned models enabling conversational back-and-forth interactions.
    • Agents: Models that recurse upon themselves with open-ended tool calls and reasoning steps rather than following prescripted linear workflows.
    • Always-On Agents ("Claws"): Agents capable of running for extended periods (minutes to hours) utilizing heartbeat mechanisms to wake up repeatedly, perform multi-turn loops, and creatively unblock themselves.
    • Orchestrating Agents: Models capable of complex, parallelized task distribution and agent-to-agent interactions to manage context windows and coordinate advanced workflows.

Real-World Fleet Deployment: Landscaping Use Case

  • End-to-End Business Automation:
    • Highlighted a real customer case where a landscaping business runs end-to-end via a fleet of HyperAgent agents handling prospecting, surveying, pitching, and quote generation.
  • Agent Workflow & Human-in-the-Loop:
    • Triager Agent: Takes inbound client inquiries, assesses project scope, and verifies lead quality.
    • Surveyor Agent: Uses a fully capable sandbox VM to manipulate files and analyze imagery/video (via tools like `ffmpeg`) to design bespoke pitch decks and interactive web pages.
    • Human Oversight: Human managers intervene only to unblock agents and authorize high-impact actions like final quotes or client pitches, serving as orchestrators of an agent fleet rather than executing micro-tasks.
    • Real-Time Learning: Agents accumulate memories, skill updates, and user feedback in real time (via Slack, email, or direct interactions) to adjust performance dynamically without waiting for retraining cycles.

HyperAgent Startup Battlefield Finals

  • Competition Details:
    • Culmination of the "Founding 500" cohort pre-selected down to 20 competitors, featuring a live pitch in front of judges Howie Liu, Theo (T3 Chat), and Joshua (HeyGen).
    • Backed by $100,000 in prizes provided by HyperAgent.
  • Finalists & Winners:
    • Comment.io: Grand prize winner ($50,000); a multiplayer markdown editor built specifically for real-time collaboration between humans and AI agents.
    • Built by Foundry: Runner-up ($30,000); deploys teams of agents to help content creators build recurring revenue businesses and launch custom products.
    • Kamad: Second runner-up ($20,000); agentifies physical commodity trade through a proprietary CFC state machine that verifies, executes, and settles global trade deals.

Notable Quotes

"The ultimate blocker to still be able to deploy useful agents there's still a human element to that... the ability for agents to get unblocked by humans that still own policies and decisions and really importantly just kind of gate... really critical high impact actions." — Howie Liu
"It's not going to be AI taking your job, it's going to be you know somebody using AI who takes your job or takes your business." — Howie Liu
"Truly nothing's defensible other than being the most creative. Like anybody these days has a product, but we actually do like the anthropic eat your lunch model. Like we like the ship extinction model where we ship and then 10 companies go extinct the next day." — Spirro Anakos
Please join me in welcoming the chief executive officer at Hyper Agent, Howy Lou. All right. Hello. Who's excited about the end of this event? This has been an amazing few days. Amazing talks. Amazing Gary talk right there. And by the way, I've never been introduced with so much base. I am very, very pumped in this moment. So, I consider myself a product thinker at heart. Meaning, you know, as much as I like to spend time on the technical side of building—and you know, I love to think about architecture and algorithms and so on—really, I'm a product form factor thinker at heart. And you know, what I want to end today off with is a brief and kind of high-level overview of how I see the world going with agents as it relates to product form factors. So, I'm calling this hiring employable agents, and it's really a reflection on: as these models have gotten better and better, and we've heard from some really, really great speakers about the latest frontier models—whether GLM 5.2 or others—and how to coax the most performance out of them. Really, this is not about the technical deep dive, but how do we think about these from a product building standpoint? Who am I? The founder and CEO of Air Tableable, and also Hyper Agent, which is a product within Air Tableable. And as many of you already know, Air Tableable is basically an almost laughably horizontal platform. When we in fact set out to build the company almost 12 years ago, we got basically this negative feedback from virtually every investor we talked to early on, which was, "You can't possibly go out with a horizontal platform approach and try to build something that is everything for everyone, like any type of app that people want to build. You can't solve all of those use cases." And I think we ended up doing a pretty good job of solving for many of them at least, and solving for a large builder community. And we're basically applying a very, very similar philosophy to agents now, and I'm going to break down how we're doing it. When I zoom out and think about the progression of models and therefore the product form factors they enable, I really see it as this spectrum. So, we went from basically completions, which is you give a model, one of these LLMs, a string of tokens and it just continues emitting more tokens until it gets to a stop token. More or less, it would just kind of complete your thought, or complete the pattern. And until InstructGPT happened and enabled a chat form factor, it was like arguably powerful, but more narrowly applicable. Once we got to a higher level of model intelligence, and then also the kind of innovation of InstructGPT and tuning these models to actually have more of a conversational back-and-forth behavior with users, then we enabled the chatbot. And of course, we all know ChatGPT was an awesome breakout product. We're now all very, very intimately familiar with that form factor of AI. The next level I call an agent, and I think agent has been very overloaded. We've probably talked about that a ton over the past few days. But my definition of agent really is the same as the Anthropic definition, which is distinct from workflows. An agent is really a model that recurses upon itself and has the open-ended set of tools that it could call, of reasoning steps it can make, of decisions it can make. So, it's not prescripted to a linear workflow, but it can kind of make an open-ended set of choices. Still, I like to think of agents as being a little bit distinct from the next level of agents, which I'm using the term claw here because OpenClaw obviously has popularized this concept. But there's a market shift that I see from agents that were still mostly reactive to user input. So, you would have an agent go and perform a task. It might spend 20 minutes, maybe up to an hour to perform the task, and then it completes. Claws, as I call them, are really about agents that go and perform work for much longer. They can even have a mechanism like the heartbeat mechanism within OpenClaw, and now in other products like Hyper Agent, that enable them to wake up on their own and almost like exhibit this always-on behavior. Underneath the hood, what's happening is each wakeup basically results in many different turns. The agent loops upon itself, it's performing tool calls, each tool call response then invokes the LLM again until it reaches a stopping point. But then that stopping point gets reinvoked, or kind of restarted, with a heartbeat mechanism where you can wake up the agent again and again. And so this idea of this always-on agent that's actually constantly moving towards its goal and figuring out creatively how to unblock itself where maybe prior agents would get stuck—I think that's a really, really important kind of next form factor leap that we're seeing. And then finally, as you think about models that are now capable of really, really intelligent orchestration of complex tasks. So, I've personally been using the workflows capability in Claude Code quite a bit, where it can fan out a lot of work to a massively parallel set of agents. But this concept of this agent that can go and orchestrate with other agents—an agent-to-agent interaction to perform even more advanced tasks, longer-running jobs—is I think the next leap yet. All of this is enabled because the models are getting smarter and smarter to the point where they can achieve coherence even on longer and more open-ended scoped problems. If you recall back to the days of BabyAGI and AutoGPT, those were really kind of fun, cool science experiments. But ultimately, as much as they were really interesting to watch and see them work for hours and hours on a problem, they would all inevitably end up drifting off to something not super useful. It would go off into a rabbit hole and kind of get stuck. And I think now the models are actually smart enough to enable not only longer-running jobs, but actually enable us to start applying these kind of organizational principles to break down problems into differently scoped tasks that different agents can hand off to each other. All of which is about managing each agent's task or context window and ability to coordinate work with each other. So all this is kind of abstract. I want to make it very, very tangible and show you how we've applied some of that design thinking of building a horizontal product and distilling a lot of the complexity of agents—like we did for building a database or a database-powered app with Airtable—for agents in Hyper Agent. This is a very realistic use case based on some of our actual customer usage. One of the interesting things we found from launching Hyper Agent is the prototypical users for Hyper Agent are not who you would think they are. I mean, we definitely get our fair share of the super AI-forward, kind of pure software companies, like AI companies themselves. But we also get really interesting other companies that are sometimes like traditionally offline businesses. And in this case, we're depicting a very realistic scenario, which is a customer that is in a landscaping business and actually uses agents to run virtually end-to-end every part of their business. Which is finding clients to propose landscaping projects to, physically going and redoing their garden or their backyard, and end-to-end managing everything from the prospecting to building a quote and a pitch to managing the work itself. And I think this is a huge, huge interesting insight that I've just kind of stumbled on from working on Hyper Agent, which is: the ingenuity of agent builders is not contained to just AI companies or software companies. And I was really inspired by Gary's talk just a second ago. I think that the motive behind that applies to every industry, every sector. And there are really, really creative people, many of whom are in this room, who can go out and disrupt your own industry, whatever it be. It doesn't have to be a pure software business. But I think all it takes is the ability to imagine what work could look like when you do go and become fully not just an AI-native company, but I like to use the term a fully fleet-of-agents-native company where you're actually going above and beyond and not just thinking about small, kind of narrow steps to automate with AI, but actually how to employ a whole fleet of agents that have governed behavior and have the right context and the right tools to be able to do really useful and very end-to-end autonomous work. So let's walk through what this looks like. This is a realistic example of: in this business, this landscaper wants to go and pitch clients. They get all these inbound submissions of clients who are interested. Somebody submits this inquiry saying, "Hey, here's my current backyard. It's a little messy. Here's my info. Can you give me a quote and show me a proposal of what you could do?" This basically gets routed into one agent that is the triager agent. And so this agent is basically taking in this intake and saying, "Hey, what is the scope of the project? Is this something we want to take on and is this a quality lead? Is this a legit person?" And so it does some research, but ultimately it hands off then the work to another agent, a surveyor agent, to actually go and then look at the physical landscape of this person's backyard and come up with a proposal of what a re-landscaped version of that could look like. Here the surveyor agent is going and looking at the original imagery or even video that the submitter gave them. Hyper Agent in our case works with a fully capable sandbox VM. So every agent, in fact every thread with an agent, is able to fully write code. It can manipulate files. It can actually use things like ffmpeg to clip out individual screenshots from a video file and then analyze those and actually go through and find all the relevant information to create a fairly high-quality and high-touch proposal that would have been unfathomable for a small business like this before. If you think about a really high-end interior designer or maybe a very high-end landscaper, maybe they could do a very bespoke proposal like this for a $5 million plus client if they're working for a big hotel or something. But it would have been unheard of to do something this high-touch and actually mock up real imagery and a real client pitch deck for just a small backyard landscaping proposal. But this person has done it because they've figured out how to use agents in an extremely capable and autonomous way to do the job that otherwise would have been undoable by humans. And so what happens next is the surveyor agent went and created this proposal and pitch, and now the intervention has to go back to the human. There is a step where humans still need to unblock the agents. Sarah Guo put out a great post a few weeks ago about just describing where the value is going to accrue in this AI landscape as things evolve and models get smarter. And I firmly agree with her take, which was the ultimate blocker to still be able to deploy useful agents—there's still a human element to that. As much as models are getting smarter, the ability for agents to get unblocked by humans that still own policies and decisions, and really importantly just kind of gate really critical high-impact actions like in this case actually making a pitch to a customer that has a binding quote or perhaps agreeing to do the work—that becomes something that the human then intervenes at, just like a great manager who's getting upwards reports or upwards work updates from their team to then unblock. So here we have Sage, who has come back to the real human owner of this business and saying, "Hey, here's a lead. I already surveyed the project. Here's a beautiful pitch deck and a proposal, and I have come up with a realistic quote based on all the factors I've already surveyed in this particular project to give to you to now approve sending to the client and see if they accept it." And you can see even the proposal itself is this really beautiful kind of interactive web page that again would have been unfathomable for a business like this to be able to send to every single client, even for small tasks or jobs like a $10,000 landscaping proposal. Better yet, as you interact with these agents, as Gary talked about with GBrain and just kind of creating this very sticky context layer that learns with every interaction, I completely agree. I think that is a universal principle and a form factor for useful agents going forward, which is agents should not be static. They should not just be either static LLM calls or static even agents that just have a prompt and some static skills. They really need to learn with every interaction. And you can't wait for the next kind of fine-tuning run of your own models where you have a bunch of collected data or rubrics to retrain the model. It should learn in real time. It should accumulate memories and skill updates and just take user feedback and actually learn from real-world performance to constantly adjust and get better just like humans do. And so in this case, you can see within Slack, or in our case within either Slack or email or other invocation methods, you can basically go back and forth with the agent just like a real human and say, "Hey, remember to take this into account next time. Here's something differently formatted or a different way to think about the proposal, or you missed this additional cost lever in this particular project. So remember that for next time." And I think it's critical that in this next generation of agents, agents need to remember these things. They should feel fluid and evolving just like humans are in order for them to fully realize the potential that the latest frontier model capabilities enable them to actually go and do in terms of autonomy. And then finally, I think what ends up happening at the end of all of this is with all of these agents that are each individually capable and even interacting with each other, I think the job of the human becomes increasingly about unblocking agents to be able to do work effectively. And so I compare this to the development workflows that have evolved from originally before any kind of AI-enabled coding—obviously, we just sat in front of a computer. We as humans are pretty single-threaded. You'd start at one code file, you'd think about a problem, you'd kind of bang out some code, maybe take a break, come back to it. To then we had augmented coding with the first generation of GitHub Copilot and similar tools, where I mean I think of that as really just a completions form factor on coding. You type some code, it can autocomplete a few extra lines. To then we had things like Cursor Composer 1.0, like the original experience, not the composer model, but the chat experience where you could talk to this agent, it would do more complex changes, and that was even more leverage. But we're now moving to this point where the best frontier agentic developers I know—and many of you are sitting in this room—are really just kind of overseeing a fleet of agents. Like in fact, going to sleep without setting off your agents to perform useful work overnight feels like you're taking this huge loss, like your team is not working because you're not unblocking. And so the UX of how you oversee these agents, whether it's in development workflows or general purpose work like this landscaping example, needs to shift as well. And so the way we've thought about that is you need to see this kind of orchestration control plane where you can see at a glance what all of your agents are working on, what they're blocked on, who's handing off work to each other. And your job becomes almost like zooming out to see the entire SimCity landscape and orchestrating across everything going on at a macro level between your different agents, rather than going and having to play micro and zoom into each individual project or each individual task. And so I think this is a really, really exciting moment as we have shifted from not just completions and the slight augmentation of existing human work, but really a radical now leap into humans as orchestrators of agents that also can orchestrate themselves. And finding the right way for us to interact with those agents as they become more and more capable is going to become not just the opportunity to boil the ocean, but in my opinion, table stakes for survival for every company in every industry. This is going to be the way to get ahead and thrive in your industry. And if you don't, as Jensen put it, it's not going to be AI taking your job, it's going to be somebody using AI who takes your job or takes your business. So really, really excited both for the opportunity and for everyone in this room to be part of this transformation and this really kind of bold leap into the next form factor of AI.
