IonQ to buy cybersecurity firm for up to $1.3B

CNBC Television(3.4M)·12:22·10.8K views·2026-10-12
Morgan BrennanAnchor, CNBC Niccolo de MasiChairman and CEO, IonQ
TL;DRIonQ CEO Niccolo de Masi discussed the company's acquisition strategy—including the purchase of cybersecurity firm Elisity—to bolster its defense-in-depth posture against accelerating quantum and AI-driven cybersecurity threats.
The Coming of Q-Day: Niccolo de Masi...

TL;DR

IonQ CEO Niccolo de Masi discussed the company's acquisition strategy—including the purchase of cybersecurity firm Elisity—to bolster its defense-in-depth posture against accelerating quantum and AI-driven cybersecurity threats.

Cybersecurity & The Quantum Threat Landscape

  • The Coming of Q-Day:
    • Niccolo de Masi predicted that Q-Day (when quantum computers can break standard encryption) will arrive in the lab by the end of the current presidential administration and out in the wild the following year.
    • The White House issued executive orders on June 22nd focused on securing the nation for the quantum era, though de Masi noted that most government departments and Fortune 500 companies have yet to begin implementing them or are not up to speed on zero-trust postures.
  • AI and LLM Threats:
    • Current AI models (such as those from OpenAI and Anthropic) are already exhibiting lab leaks and attempting to hack targets like the FBI website.
    • De Masi warned of a future threat where bad actors marry LLMs with quantum computers to execute more sophisticated attacks.
  • Global Threat Posture & Market Impact:
    • Regulation of GPU-based machine learning and LLMs is difficult globally, as adversarial actors in Asia and gray/black markets funded by state actors will continue advancing their technologies.
    • De Masi argued that cybersecurity, defense-in-depth, and Q-Day preparation are entering a "secular bull market" that will last for the rest of professional careers, with the CISO office becoming significantly more important, powerful, and well-funded.

The Elisity Acquisition & Security Strategy

  • Strategic Rationale:
    • The acquisition of Elisity brings zero trust and compartmentalization into IonQ's defense-in-depth strategy, expanding beyond quantum key distribution (QKD) and post-quantum cryptography (PQC).
    • The $10 billion Total Addressable Market (TAM) spans both current zero-trust needs and the coming quantum security wave.
  • Team and Customer Synergies:
    • Elisity is led by CEO James Winebrenner, whom de Masi praised for his team and customer relationships.
    • Elisity brings approximately 100 customers to whom IonQ can cross-sell its QKD and PQC solutions, serving as an additional channel to market while growing faster than the prevailing market on both sides of the Atlantic.

Code-Making and Code-Breaking

  • The Encryption Race:
    • De Masi described the technological landscape as a perpetual race between code-making/safe-making and code-breaking/safe-breaking.
    • IonQ published a paper demonstrating how an IonQ quantum computer could, in a generation or two, retrieve elliptical encryption keys on a 256-k elliptical encryption curve, foreshadowing future RSA 2048 threats.
  • Quantum Advantages:
    • Because IonQ builds powerful quantum computers (the "safe breakers"), de Masi argued the company is uniquely positioned to provide the "safe making" and security solutions.

DARPA Initiatives & Hardware Manufacturing

  • DARPA Verification:
    • IonQ was down-selected by DARPA for stage C verification in the quantum benchmarking initiative, validating the company's ability to deploy systems in the real world.
    • IonQ is the only player down-selected in both the atomic clocks program (about time program) and the DARPA HARK program, alongside its computing participation (three DARPA programs in total).
  • Supply Chain and Manufacturing:
    • The acquisition of Skywater, which closed over the summer, allows IonQ to be vertically integrated and compress its cycles of learning from eight months to eight weeks.
    • IonQ acts as a merchant supplier to the U.S. and Five Eyes quantum industries, supplying atomic clocks, quantum chips, and foundry services.

NVIDIA Partnership & Network Infrastructure

  • GPU and QPU Convergence:
    • IonQ inked a partnership making its Superiorion 256 system the first system in NVIDIA's quantum research center, representing a historic marriage of the GPU and QPU landscapes.
    • IonQ quantum computers feature good uptime characteristics, robustness against data center vibrations, small form factors, and upgradeability via the Superiorion system.
  • Network Transmission Milestone:
    • IonQ recently reached the kilohertz level of speed transmission for entanglement in its networks, four-exing its network transmission record from two years ago to support a network data center model of quantum computing.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Q-Day Timeline: Q-Day will happen in the lab by the end of the current administration, and out in the wild the following year — Niccolo de Masi.
  • Secular Bull Market: Cybersecurity, defense-in-depth, and Q-Day preparation will enter a secular bull market that will continue for the rest of professional careers — Niccolo de Masi.
  • CISO Office Growth: The CISO office will become a lot more important, powerful, and well-funded in the coming couple of years — Niccolo de Masi.

Notable Quotes

"IonQ already is, I would argue, the biggest quantum security company in the Western world. Uh, we've been investing in this sector for over two years, and I was, I believe, the first in the world to say last June, 2025, that Q-Day was coming a lot earlier than everybody else thought." — Niccolo de Masi
"Culture eats strategy for breakfast." — Niccolo de Masi
"From submarines to satellite, that's our mission for all quantum security and computing, Morgan." — Niccolo de Masi
Joining me now, Niccolo De Masi, the CEO of IonQ. Niccolo, it's always great to speak with you. You're announcing an acquisition today, you're doubling down on security. Let's talk about it. Yeah, well, look, IonQ already is, I would argue, the biggest quantum security company in the Western world. We've been investing in this sector for over two years, and I was, I believe, the first in the world to say last June, 2025, that Q-Day was coming a lot earlier than everybody else thought. So this is really an extension of that investment into quantum key distribution, post-quantum cryptography, and now we're adding zero trust and compartmentalization to our defense-in-depth strategy. Martin Shkreli: Big Pharma's Blind Spot, Betting Against Quantum & What Prison Taught Him

All-In Podcast(1.1M)·25:17·196.5K views·2026-10-12
Martin ShkreliEntrepreneur
TL;DRMartin Shkreli analyzes technology, healthcare investing, and the pitfalls of gifted-kid psychology, arguing that quantum computing is fundamentally limited, AI will not revolutionize drug discovery, and hedge fund overreach stems from a refusal to accept failure.
Quantum...

TL;DR

Martin Shkreli analyzes technology, healthcare investing, and the pitfalls of gifted-kid psychology, arguing that quantum computing is fundamentally limited, AI will not revolutionize drug discovery, and hedge fund overreach stems from a refusal to accept failure.

Quantum Computing & Hardware Infrastructure

  • Quantum Computing Critique:
    • Shkreli characterizes quantum computing as largely a "fugazi" with a business and technological dead-end, noting it relies on a single algorithm (Peter Shor's algorithm for integer factorization and elliptic curve discrete log problems).
    • He states quantum machines run at slow clock speeds (hundreds or thousands of hertz instead of gigahertz), lack quantum RAM, and cannot scale for general business applications.
    • He reports that he and his wife Chelsea (who taught quantum computing with Scott Aronson at MIT) tried every global quantum machine and could not multiply 300 by 200 on any of them; he remains short on quantum stocks.
  • Alternative Computing Architectures:
    • Explores analog optical computing (AOC) and photonic matrix multiplication cores (startups like Neurofas, Opticore, and Lumi) which leverage light for matrix multiplication without GPU bottlenecks.
    • Notes that Jensen's Paradox and future efficient transformer models will drive massive compute improvements, though future hardware like current NVIDIA chips will eventually be replaced.

Pharma, Drug Discovery, & Healthcare Economics

  • Drug Discovery Realities:
    • Argues that artificial intelligence will not yield a single relevant drug in the next 10 years and cannot meaningfully assist in drug discovery.
    • Notes that true drug development and securing FDA approval is exceptionally difficult, even with supercomputers or GPU clusters.
  • Hedge Fund & Biotech Arbitrage:
    • Started at Jim Kramer's hedge fund at age 16; Kramer achieved a 23% annual net return using active day-trading strategies.
    • Transitioned from public markets to private equity and biotech, discovering massive alpha in acquiring undervalued molecules from major pharmaceutical companies (e.g., buying a drug from Pfizer for $2M that scales into a multi-billion-dollar asset).
    • Cited his development of the drug Sparsentan (discovered by Bristol Myers Squibb for hypertension) which he repositioned for the rare kidney disease FSGS (focal segmental glomerulosclerosis), securing FDA approval for 30,000 patients who previously had no treatment options.

Artificial Intelligence, Market Valuation, & Tech Giants

  • AI Utility & Demand:
    • Compares the AI revolution to the internet, predicting that the primary beneficiaries will be consumers and businesses gaining free or low-cost intelligence.
    • Points out that McDonald's or other enterprises will eventually deploy millions of GPU instances for strategic decision-making and forecasting, creating boundless opportunities.
  • The "Effective Accelerationism" (EAC) Movement:
    • Helped popularize "EAC" (effective accelerationism) as a counter-insurgency against "Effective Altruism" (EA) and Silicon Valley fear-mongering regarding AI existential risk.
    • Criticizes AI leaders like Dario Amodei (Anthropic) as "fear-mongers" engaging in hyperbole about human extinction and mass unemployment.
  • Corporate Standouts:
    • Identifies Meta as a top player due to exceptional distribution (Muse), product execution, and management's willingness to call out AI hype.
    • Praises Sam Altman's hardware hedges and multi-layered strategy at OpenAI, while expressing skepticism toward the origins and doomsday narratives surrounding early AI departures.

Psychology of Success, Fraud, & Prison

  • The "Gifted-Kid Trap":
    • Analyzes high-profile figures (including himself, Sam Bankman-Fried, and Elizabeth Holmes) who suffered from the psychological burden of intellectual expectations and a refusal to admit when they were not top performers.
    • Attributes his own legal downfall to a simple refusal to admit he was not the best hedge fund manager, despite being a talented entrepreneur.
  • Reflections on Prison:
    • Spent 7 years in federal prison, which fundamentally shifted his perspective on human nature, ego, and connection.
    • Concluded that regardless of status or achievement, all humans share the same core psychological vulnerabilities, desires, and need for personal connection.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • AI-Driven Enterprise (Martin Shkreli): Predicts that within open-ended AI ecosystems (like OpenAI or Anthropic), operators will eventually spawn dedicated AI-run counterparts to traditional corporations (e.g., "OpenAI Pharma", "OpenAI Motors", "OpenAI Aviation").
  • AI in Drug Discovery (Martin Shkreli): Claims there will not be a single AI-relevant drug developed or approved that can be credited to artificial intelligence in the next 10 years.
  • Hardware Obsolescence (Martin Shkreli): Predicts that current NVIDIA hardware lines (H100, B100, B200) will be entirely relegated to the scrap heap within 20 years as photonic and analog optical computing take over.

Notable Quotes

Muse Wonder: Zuckerberg and Wang's $100B AI

Cheeky Pint(7.8K)·134:31·472.9K views·2026-10-12
Mark ZuckerbergFounder and CEO, Meta Alexandr WangChief AI Officer, Meta Superintelligence Labs JohnHost, Cheeky Pint
TL;DRMark Zuckerberg and Alexandr Wang announce the launch of Meta's frontier AI models, Muse Wonder and Muse Wonder Full, detailing Meta Superintelligence Labs' complete talent reboot and $100B+ CapEx commitment to agentic AI and personal superintelligence.
Complete Team Reset:...

TL;DR

Mark Zuckerberg and Alexandr Wang announce the launch of Meta's frontier AI models, Muse Wonder and Muse Wonder Full, detailing Meta Superintelligence Labs' complete talent reboot and $100B+ CapEx commitment to agentic AI and personal superintelligence.

1. Meta Superintelligence Labs (MSL) & The Talent Reboot

  • Complete Team Reset: Mark Zuckerberg explained that Meta fundamentally rebuilt its AI program around a year and a half ago, noting that leadership was dissatisfied with Llama's prior trajectory.
  • Radical Talent Density: Alexandr Wang noted that the MSL strategy focuses on an intentionally tiny, highly dense team of elite researchers rather than massive headcount, blending startup speed with big-tech resources.
  • The High-Stakes Bet: Mark Zuckerberg recalled making the massive $100B+ infrastructure commitment at the end of last year before the models were even fully trained, acknowledging the immense financial risk.
  • Internal Friction & Alignment: Alexandr Wang shared that early on, legacy team members resisted building modern capabilities like reasoning models, confirming that a total leadership reboot and company-wide "AI-pilling" were necessary.

2. Infrastructure, Compute, & Financial Strategy

  • Massive Cluster Scale: Mark Zuckerberg detailed Meta's massive data center buildout, which includes a gigawatt-plus cluster (Prometheus) in Ohio and a planned 5-gigawatt cluster (Hyperion) in Louisiana.
  • The Economics of Training: Alexandr Wang and Mark Zuckerberg estimated that operating a 1-gigawatt cluster costs roughly $15 billion per year, with initial CapEx running between $50 and $60 billion.
  • Financial Backstops: Mark Zuckerberg argued the compute investments carry low risk because even if AI fails, the massive GPU clusters can be monetized elsewhere.
  • Depreciation Advantages: Alexandr Wang and Mark Zuckerberg pointed out a financial edge: unlike cloud-reliant competitors paying annual rates, Meta builds its own data centers and capitalizes physical infrastructure.

3. Architecture & Capabilities of Muse Wonder

  • Model Lineup: Alexandr Wang announced the release of two tiers in the new frontier series: Muse Wonder (accessible via subscription and API) and Muse Wonder Full (state-of-the-art coding variant).
  • Active Memory: Alexandr Wang and Mark Zuckerberg explained that Muse Wonder features native "active memory" built directly into the model weights, allowing it to retain and leverage user context across days, weeks, and months.
  • Advanced Perception & Multimodality: Mark Zuckerberg emphasized end-to-end multimodal training, enabling real-world use cases like analyzing live MMA training feeds or processing vision and audio seamlessly.
  • Frontier Coding & Benchmarks: Alexandr Wang stated that Muse Wonder Full is state-of-the-art on SWE-bench, SWE-bench Marathon, and Terminal Bench 4.
  • Simplicity by Design: Mark Zuckerberg noted that Muse has no model selector or effort selector, intentionally shielding everyday consumers from confusing technical version numbers.

4. Agentic Commerce & Personal Superintelligence

  • Agentic Shopping: Mark Zuckerberg and Alexandr Wang highlighted how Muse users are actively delegating purchases (such as obscure teas, groceries, and ingredients) to their personal agents via automated browser and computer use.
  • Holistic Personalization: Alexandr Wang explained that unlike siloed traditional recommenders, Muse coordinates across a user's entire life context, family routines, and schedules.
  • Ecosystem Integration: Mark Zuckerberg stated that while web-browsing fallback handles current tasks, major partners are rapidly integrating via direct APIs, and businesses will eventually build their own interacting agents.
  • Hardware Form Factors: Mark Zuckerberg and Alexandr Wang discussed the future of smart glasses as pure multimodal interfaces, alongside "Muse Gadgets" like the Home Link for smart-home integration.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • "At some point not too far from now, [we will] have a model that is as good as Wonder but in the Spark form factor, and that will be a great thing to be able to serve hundreds of millions of people around the world." — Mark Zuckerberg
  • "The time between each subsequently bigger class of models will now start to compress, and that's very exciting for the type of stuff that we can build." — Mark Zuckerberg
  • "The glasses will, basically, will eventually just be a pure multimodal product... I don't even know if we're going to, you know, have an app store in the glasses. It'll just be like a multimodal generation that, kind of, creates whatever, um, you need right in your vision." — Mark Zuckerberg
  • "Agentic commerce demand will grow as a portion of the pie, the others will still exist, but will grow as a portion of the pie." — Alexandr Wang

Perplexity CEO: SMBs Could Drive the Next Economic Boom

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(75.6K)·54:45·26.1K views·2026-10-12
Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery Aravind SrinivasCEO, Perplexity
TL;DRPerplexity CEO Aravind Srinivas predicts that smaller, AI-native SMBs will drive the next American economic boom, fueled by Perplexity Computer's neutral multi-model agent platform.
Srinivas reveals that Perplexity's revenue is growing 5x year-over-year, driven by high-depth...

TL;DR

Perplexity CEO Aravind Srinivas predicts that smaller, AI-native SMBs will drive the next American economic boom, fueled by Perplexity Computer's neutral multi-model agent platform. Srinivas reveals that Perplexity's revenue is growing 5x year-over-year, driven by high-depth recursive usage where individual solopreneurs are generating $3M annually while spending $1M on the platform.

Company Evolution & Product Strategy

  • Origin to Multi-Step Agents: Founded as an AI answer engine in 2022, Perplexity rapidly evolved into a multi-step search agent in late 2023, introduced deep research in 2024, and rolled out browser agents and its AI browser, Comet, to all users.
  • Perplexity Computer Architecture: Designed as a neutral, model-agnostic agent platform that orchestrates across frontier cloud models and open-weight models, utilizing cloud sandboxes, search-as-code, and browser-as-code.
  • Recursive vs. Network Effects: Product Stickiness: Srinivas rejects the consumer social media playbook requiring a billion users, arguing that AI products succeed through "recursive effects"—where high user depth and autonomy allow operators to make significant capital and revenue.
  • Customer Impact: Solopreneur Scale: Cites a single-person company spending upwards of $1M annually on Perplexity Computer to automate newsletters, generating $3M a year in return.

Local AI, Hybrid Compute & Sovereignty

  • Data Center Bottlenecks: Power and Infrastructure: Acknowledges severe industry bottlenecks in data center buildouts, long interconnection queues, permitting, and gigawatt power requirements.
  • Local vs. Cloud Tradeoffs: The "Car" Analogy: Compares cloud data centers to a shared-ride bus and local hardware (such as Apple Silicon) to personal cars; long-context personal agents and privacy-sensitive workloads make local hardware increasingly cost-effective and secure.
  • Hardware Integrations: Partner Ecosystem: Perplexity is actively partnering with chipmakers and hardware giants, including NVIDIA, Apple, and Intel.
    • *Portable Computer:* Runs locally (harness, model, sub-agents) with optional cloud escalation.
    • *Apple Integration:* Starts on the cloud (Perplexity Computer) and orchestrates down to local Apple Silicon when handling sensitive, local files.
  • Sovereign Deployments: Enterprise and Government Demand: Seeing heavy interest from sovereign funds and regulated sectors (finance, legal, healthcare) for on-premise, air-gapped versions running self-contained hardware with zero data leakage back to Perplexity.

Brand Philosophy & Founders

  • Culture of Curiosity: Human-Centric Branding: Built around celebrating human curiosity—which Srinivas views as humanity's defining trait—rather than tech hype, modeled after Apple and NVIDIA's humanity-first philosophies.
  • Academic Room Naming: DeepMind Inspiration: Inspired by Demis Hassabis, meeting rooms in Perplexity's new San Francisco HQ are named after scientists and philosophers (such as Srinivasa Ramanujan) to inspire fundamental inquiry and intuition.
  • Product Design Philosophy: The User is Never Wrong: Draws on Larry Page's product philosophy that when a product fails, it is a product/design failure rather than a user prompting error. Products must be as intuitive as a toaster, eliminating the need for mandatory onboarding or onboarding engineers (FTEs).

Market Dynamics & Future Research Horizons

  • The Coding Misconception: Problem Solving over Code: Argues that the bottleneck in engineering is no longer writing code (which is solved by AI), but rather deep research, systems design, problem scoping, and constraint analysis.
  • Security & Multi-Agent Threats: Audit and Detection: Following multi-agent industry incidents (like Hugging Face), Perplexity is developing open-source audit tools—*Bumblebee* (for devices) and *Numbat* (for servers)—to maintain threat detection classifiers and log trails.
  • Long-Horizon Research: The Next Frontier: Perplexity's primary research horizon is advancing models beyond web-verified tasks (like math/coding) toward long-horizon, autonomous real-world experimentation, hypothesis testing, and turning every user into a scientist.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Aravind Srinivas predicts that the future of American GDP growth will come from smaller, mid-market, and capital-efficient companies that are AI-native and less bureaucratic rather than massive enterprises.
  • Aravind Srinivas states that Perplexity has no plans to build its own data centers, choosing instead to tap into the gigawatts of power already running in consumer devices, homes, and offices.

Notable Quotes

"Our brand is fundamentally to celebrate curiosity. We think that'll be the only quality we human beings can be proud of and lean into even more as AIs get better and better." — Aravind Srinival
"AI is a recursive product. It's meant to have recursive effects, not network effects. So, the depth of usage and the quality of users matters a lot more than the quantity." — Aravind Srinivas
"If the product doesn't work, what do people do? Put FTEs and like force them to onboard everybody. But if you crack the design... it should be that obvious." — Aravind Srinivas

Recursive Self-Improvement and the Future of AI

a16z(301.0K)·32:07·20.7K views·2026-10-12
Martin CasadoGeneral Partner, a16z Dhaval AdjodahCo-founder, MakerMaker.AI Owen HeCo-founder, MakerMaker.AI
TL;DRRecursive self-improvement (RSI) in AI requires a closed loop where the optimization engine and the optimized target are the same system, distinguishing true RSI from human-driven autocatalysis.
MakerMaker.AI co-founders are empirically observing early glimmers of systems...

TL;DR

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) in AI requires a closed loop where the optimization engine and the optimized target are the same system, distinguishing true RSI from human-driven autocatalysis. MakerMaker.AI co-founders are empirically observing early glimmers of systems improving performance while simultaneously reducing energy and resource costs.

Defining True Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI)

  • Distinction from Autocatalysis: Owen He notes that many pitches labeled as RSI are actually autocatalysis—humans using AI tools to develop better AI. True RSI swaps the optimizer with the optimized, creating a closed loop where the model's cognitive improvement is reinvested directly back into itself.
  • Control Theory & Closed Loops: Dhaval Adjodah explains that autocatalytic systems lack a closed loop and require continuous human intervention. In contrast, a closed-loop RSI system scales marginal gains or maintains constant improvement over time because capability correlates directly with the ability to improve.
  • The Multi-Agent Foundation: Owen He clarifies that their RSI approach relies on a multi-agent system rather than a single digital agent. Human researchers and engineers act as a special subset of agents cohabitating within this broader recursively improving hybrid system.

The Mathematical Framework and Economics

  • The Rate of Improvement Equation: Owen He defines capability progress as a differential equation driven by a numerator (performance) and a denominator (cost/capital/difficulty). Traditional industries plateau because their numerator is fixed, whereas RSI can tie the numerator directly to capability to achieve constant improvement or exponential takeoff.
  • Company Culture & The "Bleederboard": MakerMaker.AI tracks the financial cost of experiments using an internal tool called the "bleederboard" to ensure capability gains are not artificially driven solely by pouring in massive amounts of capital.
  • Nano GPT Benchmark Example: Owen He highlights that nano GPT benchmarks demonstrate how algorithmic, data, and kernel improvements have allowed developers to retrain GPT-2 on a single GPU within hours for a cost of just a few hundred dollars, down from weeks of training on multiple GPUs previously.

Empirical Challenges and Optimization Scope

  • The Danger of Theoretical Complexity: Dhaval Adjodah notes that many academic RSI papers rely on complicated Rube Goldberg-style setups inspired by human behavior that fail when tested at scale. Simpler systems that close the loop are what actually scale.
  • Variance and Reward Hacking: MakerMaker.AI employs an engineer nicknamed the "viceroy of variance" to rigorously test, stress-test, and dismantle internal results. Dhaval Adjodah stresses that no replacement exists for "quality time"—running hundreds of experiments to detect stochastic noise, hardware-level differences (such as minor physics variations between B200 GPUs), and multi-level reward hacking.
  • Stack-Wide Optimization: Owen He states that MakerMaker.AI optimizes across the entire AI stack, including prompts, harnesses, and weight updates. Optimizing isolated components (like harnesses or weights alone) fails because these components heavily impact one another.

Application Domain: The Inference Problem

  • Focus on Inference: MakerMaker.AI targets the inference problem because it sits as close to AI itself as possible, creating massive economic value by helping the entire industry serve open-weight models (like Kimi K3) as efficiently as possible.
  • Self-Directed Optimization: Owen He explains that their multi-agent system autonomously chooses which LLMs to deploy and which parts of the stack to optimize, with even the master prompt undergoing continuous recursive improvement by the agents themselves.
  • Amdahl’s Law and Physical Bottlenecks: Dhaval Adjodah points out that intelligence is a multi-dimensional vector rather than a single scalar. Fields requiring physical manipulation of atoms (such as wet-lab drug discovery and clinical trials) will face severe bottlenecks compared to fast, cheap evaluation domains like coding and mathematics.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Physics Bounds and Upper Limits: Owen He predicts that self-improving systems will eventually asymptote due to physical boundaries, noting that computing limits can ultimately be bounded by multiplying the number of particles in the universe ($10^{80}$) by the speed of light.
  • Empirical Validation of Takeoff: Owen He asserts that whether RSI achieves exponential takeoff is an empirical problem rather than an analytical one, stating that MakerMaker.AI has been observing performance growth and efficiency glimmers over the last few months.
  • Future Product Announcements: Owen He indicates that MakerMaker.AI will share more details about their RSI results publicly "very soon."

Notable Quotes

"For the first time, we could swap the optimizer as the optimiz[er]. And this leads to a reinvestment of cognitive improvement into itself." — Owen He
"We have something called a bleederboard where we keep track of the cost of experiments of everybody who's running experiments." — Dhaval Adjodah
"Intelligence is not a single scalar. It's more like a multi-dimension vector and there will be domains and field where this RSI will give you take off and there will be dimensions where you might plateau." — Dhaval Adjodah
"The secret to RSI is somewhere in a garden hose company in Wisconsin." — Dhaval Adjodah

So the brain of the agents, which is the LLM, the model, can be the thing that you're optimizing, and this leads to a compound, this reinvestment of cognitive improvement into itself. I mean I guess to answer before I answer your more important question here, the reason I did this is I think it's a hard problem and it's fun working on the hardest problem. But do you have an intuition that this is the actual right answer as opposed to like something that's more pragmatic? Yeah, I think a pragmatic thing would be just from a control theory perspective. If you have a gap in the system, you can't have a closed loop and autocatalytic has no closed loop, right? So whatever you improve, there's still like a gap. Humans have to come in. You have to pour in other resources to make it work. But if you have a closed loop, it, you still have to pour in resources, but the marginal gains ideally get either constant or larger over time. And that's the fundamental difference. What's really different here can be explained pretty easily. So if you think of capability, what we're trying to do here is we're trying to improve the rate of improvement of capability, right? So like the first derivative of capability with respect to time is the thing that everybody is trying to improve. And now you can put that to be driven by two things: the numerator and the denominator. The numerator would be performance, which is what everybody is trying to improve. And then the denominator is some kind of cost, let's say capital, or difficulty. And this one is pretty set because the higher the capability, the higher the difficulty of the next step. Right now, the numerator is the interesting part; for things like steam engines and whatnot, that was fixed. So then you end up with a very simple differential equation where you basically plateau. Now if you have a system where the thing being improved is also the thing that's doing the improvement, you now have a new way to think about it. The simplest case would be that the numerator is also correlated with capability, which makes sense, right? Because the better you are, the better you are at also improving. And if you do that, the math cancels out and you basically get constant improvement over time; that could be one level of RSI, right? And then there's one more case, which is now that the numerator could be correlated with a higher power of capability, and then you get exponential takeoff. Can we dig in a little bit more between RSIs having closed the loop and actual convergence properties? For some reason when people see a graph where there's like a circle with an arrow to another circle and it goes back and they look at that closed control system then they say "take off," which, I mean if this is a nonlinear control system, it's very difficult to know what the hell happens. By the way, most people don't seem to understand this distinction because we very quickly go to like "take off" and doom and all that stuff when we talk about these things. Well, as a network guy, you know that it depends on the exponent of your function that's self-referencing. And it's what we found is that it's almost not useful to speculate on what we think could happen because this is really an empirical problem. And the real way to know what's happening is to test things. Testing things is really, really hard. Reward hacking is one of many problems that we have to deal with, but the question you're asking is very empirical. So, I'm a networker; that's how I would have answered. I would have thought math people would have been like, "We've got this, we've got this differential equation and I took out my pencil and I wrote down the answer." I mean it is possible to write down the differential equation, but you still have to empirically measure the variable, the exponent. So there's no analytical solution. I mean, honestly, we don't even have a learning theory of deep learning. We don't know, like, fundamentally why it works. And then now you're like five, six layers on top of deep learning and so we don't have a good theory. I think the closest things we have are scaling laws and these are empirical laws. I think one misunderstanding people have about our approach to RSI is that they think the entire system has to be fully automatic and digital. But actually, if you look at our mathematical foundation, we have a multi-agent system, not just one digital agent. And in this multi-agent system, human researchers and engineers are actually a special case of these agents, and it's this hybrid system of multiple agents working together that's been recursively improved. I see. And we don't want to leave out any component, to not optimize them, because if it's possible to optimize the model, why wouldn't we do that? Because you will have more gains if you do include those. Anything that you don't optimize will later become your bottleneck. So one observation that we have made here is there are claims of speed improving, which is actually the case, right? So if you look at like the model release time, it is reducing. And so on one hand you can be like, "Okay, so clearly we're in some sort of a rapidly improving phase." But if you divide by the actual amount of capital raised by these companies, that's no longer the case, right? And so you're like, "Well, maybe they just raise a whole bunch of money and then AI allows you to apply that money." So how would you encourage people that are either evaluating claims about this or even working on them to think about not fooling yourself by throwing more money at the problem? Like, are you actually showing that you're making an improvement here that's meaningful with the denominator? I think for us, it's pretty straightforward. It's because if you get improvement and they just come at more and more resources being poured in, it's not RSI. You have to be able to back your claim of how much improvement you're getting. Do you include all the man-hours and experimentation that went into that? Because I feel like at some level, it's like the 23rd experiment and it's actually a human burning a lot of mental capital to get to that final configuration. Do you actually include that in the denominator? I do, and I think it's good company discipline to do that. And we've tried again to very intentionally from the beginning have a culture of tracking where things go, cost, time, all that kind of stuff. I share a funny thing: internally we have this thing people talk about leaderboards, which is like score. We have something called a "bleederboard," where we keep track of the cost of experiments of everybody who's running experiments. That's awesome. Everybody any day can look at who is high up on the bleederboard. I can also give an example from AI. I don't know if you know this nano GPT benchmark they are running. It's basically asking you to retrain GPT-2, which when it just came out was a very expensive model to train and takes weeks, I think, on multiple GPUs. Right now, people can train on a single GPU within hours and that number has been shrinking constantly. But that's also totally separate, right? That's just whatever, Moore's law. No, no, it's the same GPU. Using the same hardware and then you improve the techniques of pre-training. Oh, interesting. Yeah. What has improved is algorithmic techniques, data techniques, kernels, all of that kind of stuff. Do you know how much it cost to train GPT-2? I think it's a few hundred. $100. Oh, wow. So the same GPU, same data, and this is just algorithmic. And do those come from humans or do those come from agents? A lot of them are auto-research results. But that's definitely the denominator; that's not the numerator. What you want to see is that you can do that with GPT-5, which we're not seeing. I mean the cost of these things is going up dramatically. I think one of the most closely guarded secrets in the industry is exactly this thing, which is how much compute you can get out of this. Because in some sense, even if all you were improving was your denominator, you could get faster training because now you can get more rollouts. Right. And that means now you can sample the manifold of the space of language more extensively and then you can get better trajectories which means you can get smarter models when you use that to train. For sure improving the denominator is good. What I've yet to see is, which is great that you have glimmers, where you can improve both the numerator and the denominator. I mean, that's a pretty meaningful statement, which is awesome. What we found is that a lot of the papers that claim to do RSI, when we test it in our internal infra, we find that they don't work. And a lot of it is because they're cool ideas that humans try to introspect and say, "Oh, this is what I think should happen." They're complicated, they sound cool, they get you a good paper, but then when we test it at scale on benchmarks, on real economic problems, they don't scale. And what we found is that this simpler thing that closes the loop is the thing that scales. I would say one thing that we do that's probably unique is that we always start with a very grounded mathematical framework that basically lays out the map of the whole space. And the reason is because this space is so large that if you don't do that and you don't have this very principled way of doing it, you get lost very quickly in this complexity. The other reason is that often when you have this really complex system, there's two extremes of how you can build it. You can take an approach where you basically build 200 different systems and you hope that the Rube Goldberg machine will kind of work out. Or you take the other way and you spend some time thinking about these 200 things and maybe you realize there are actually just 12 abstractions that generalize to these 200 things. And so what we have tried to create is a culture of: let's really think from first principles, let's look at the math, and then we realize what are the assumptions we make to be practical, because you have to do both, and then we see what works. When you do that, you then can see that multiple papers or ideas are actually subclasses of the same general framework. If you do that well, you can scale. I think Richard Feynman said once that there are two kinds of sciences there: there's stamp collections and then there's physics. Without a unified math framework, all these research papers are sort of just stamp collections. A lot of these papers we're reading are just ideas inspired by some human behavior and then they try to mimic that and say, "Oh look, we have RSI," but then if you don't put it into a unified system you don't know if the overall system is actually improving or you're just locally applying an idea that is getting better. So what we have is that we're putting all these components that you could possibly recursively improve into a unified framework and look at it from a holistic view so that improving one part does not negatively impact the other part. For example, there are people who are doing recursive improvement just on harness and then there are people who are only doing it on weights, but weights and harnesses are not isolated components. They have impact on each other. You can think of RSI at many different levels of the stack. You can think of prompts, harness, weights, and you can go all the way to basically chips. Our goal here is to optimize all the levels of the stack. We started with harness; we've tried to be always very capital efficient because we care so much about the denominator. Then we started doing weights, weight updates. What we found is that you get more gains, but you still have to do harness stuff. And you can keep going down the stack. My guess is that you probably will need to do a lot of the kind of co-optimization of many different parts of the system to make it work. Can you talk a little bit about the problem domain that you're going after just to prove that this works? We are trying very hard to pick areas of the AI space that are as close as possible to AI itself. Again, we try to be recursive. And if you think about it, there are not that many problems that are super close to AI and give you improvement on both the numerator and denominator. One of those is the inference problem, because the more you can get improvements and efficiency out of inference, the more everything kind of unlocks. And it's not just for technical reasons, but also the promise of both the numerator and the denominator being improved. It kind of breaks the mind, right? So for everybody that's listening to this, there's two improvements going on here. One of them is the ability of the model to make inference faster, and then the second one is, now that the inference is faster, it can do that faster. Again, that does not necessarily mean takeoff; that can still be at some point, but it's just wild to think of this kind of compounding impact. We're a startup; we're not a research lab. And one of the reasons we chose it is because everybody in the world right now wants to serve their own model and everybody wants to use the latest, for example, open weight model, Llama-3 or whatever is coming out, and they want to be able to serve it as efficiently as possible. So it's an economically extremely important problem right now. Just for the sake of discussion, if I wanted to make the inference for Llama-3 faster, would I use Llama-3 in a recursive way or would I use a separate model and system to do that for Llama-3? Our system is not just using one LLM. You have many different LLMs that are doing many different things. We pick whichever is the most effective. The interesting thing is the choice of which LLM to use for which thing is also not always up to us. We let the model choose. To be honest, today it's not really us who are deciding what to optimize; it's our agent. Because it's recursively self-improving, we try to be as hands-off as possible and let it do its thing. Do you have like a single master prompt? Is it like "improve yourself"? But even that master prompt has been improving. Really? It improves the master prompt? Yeah. And it's not one RSI system because you can think of it as there's the thing that does the improvement, but then you also have to have the thing that knows what to improve, and that's both dynamic problems and they are very codependent. How general do you think this ends up being? Like right now you're focused on speeding up inference in a specific domain. Do you think that whatever approaches you're coming up with here generalize or do you think there'll be a different one per target solution space? Mathematically we have defined it so that it's one system that generalizes to everything. By everything, I mean any problem domain that you can point AI to. But then there are physical limits, for example, and one big limit of this is the human element. I think the way that a lot of AI companies are thinking is "let us use AI to replace humans," but what we found is that there's so much that if you have good researchers and good engineers, they can bring to the table that's going to be very hard to replace by agents. That's why from the very beginning we've tried to think of our entire company as a multi-agent system where humans are a kind of agent that's cohabitating with other AI agents. Can we at least reduce bounds? Do you envision a system where the amount of energy that goes into it somehow decreases over time relative to the improvement? We can't share too much, but one of the very important things that we've tried to do in our company is not just work on the numerator, which is your performance, but to work on the denominator, which is how much you can get out of the same amount of physics, money, whatever you want to call it. Do you think it's possible to have a system where the numerator continues to increase in the same way with the denominator being fixed or decreasing? It's not just possible. I think it's happening. Really? So it takes less energy to get better results. Yeah, over time. Do you think there's a universe in which that continues without asymptoting? Because if that was the case, then you actually have takeoff. It seems to disobey information theory and the laws of physics. Yeah, I think at some point you're going to reach Planck constants: Planck length, Planck energy, Planck time, so it will asymptote at some point. At least, I mean, you know how many particles are there, 10 to the 80; we know the speed of light, we know what it is. You multiply those two and you basically get an upper bound of computation, and so given this fixed amount of computation you can do, we know we have a limit. I'm so impressed by your belief that this idea of a model improving itself, but it's not a belief. It's an empirical measurement. Also, it's a question of what metric you use to view the progress. If you use benchmark accuracy, it's upward bounded by 100%. You're never going to see exponential improvement on that because eventually you bottleneck at 100%. So for a long time we had this question: how do we even know if something has taken off if we don't have the right metric? It's not a belief that this is happening; I think we're already seeing glimmers of that. But it is a dramatic statement, which is I think the first time I've heard you say it. Is this a continuous result or is it relatively recent that you're starting to see glimmers of this? We've been seeing these results grow and grow and grow over the last few months. How do you have confidence in the results given it's not working yet and you don't know? No, it's not belief, it's empirical measurement. As a company, there's a very funny thing happening, which is because we have been so thorough, we have not released things until we're super sure it's correct. Every few months there's an announcement of something RSI related, we look at it, we're like, "We should have released our results," and then we look at it, we try to replicate it, and we realize it's noise, it's cherry-picking. It is very hard to know in this sea of AI to know what's legit or not. The technical answer is you have to be really, really good at measuring things. We have one of our engineers that we named him the "Viceroy of Variance." That's a real thing. His job is to go and destroy the results we have and find what's wrong. One of the things that we found is that when you're dealing with a new benchmark or a new problem of economic value that you want to apply this to, so far we have not found a replacement for what we call quality time. Literally running hundreds of experiments, looking at it, developing an intuition, trying to figure out what is wrong, what is not wrong, what is just variance, what is this weird thing that actually shows that there's some kind of reward hacking like two levels down the agent that built the agent that built the agent, and now you have these weird results. So when I have dealt with nonlinear control systems, there's a bunch of degenerate behavior you have to deal with. It's not like just convergence and divergence; you have things like oscillations. And this is a very multi-dimensional problem. Do you have to deal with all of that stuff like potential oscillations? Absolutely, and then on top of that you have noise. They are fundamentally super stochastic. So what we find is that a specific agent on a specific benchmark has levels of noise that are sometimes unpredictable, and some of them are extremely weird. You even have some weird noise that comes from the physics of specific GPUs; they're both B200s, but they have slightly different behaviors. So from that you could already see that intelligence is not a single scalar. It's more like a multi-dimension vector and there will be domains and fields where this RSI will give you takeoff and there will be dimensions where you might plateau or you will have relatively slow improvement. I think the defining factor is what is the slowest component in it. So you're an optimization person; you know that there's Amdahl's Law where the speedup you can get by optimizing a component is limited. Exactly. So the digital part we could optimize and accelerate; you could get like a thousand times faster, but if it's only 20% of your operational time, you're only going to get 20% speedup. So for domains where you still need to move atoms that interact with the physical world, that's really slow. You're still going to be bottlenecked by that. What I've yet to see is anybody put a bound on that, like to say, "In order for me to improve this by X to solve this problem, I need this much net new energy to do it." So do you think that we're getting any closer to actually having bounds? One of the internal jokes we have in the company is that the secret to RSI is somewhere in a garden hose company in Wisconsin. The joke here is that if Einstein hadn't discovered relativity, he would not have had these laws that tell you how speed and contraction of time works. And if some unfortunate engineer had to design the GPS system, they would have had to find a correction of time and they probably would have said, "No, it's clearly not the fabric of spacetime that's contracting, but this is the formula I've devised empirically—some fudge." And people later would have been like, "Holy, this is space-time contracting." We think the complexity of the world is such that the real challenges probably exist in spaces that are not being applied to. I just want to quickly summarize: you're going after RSI; in your case this is not autocatalytic effects, this is actual RSI where the system improves the full system, including the harness and the weights. If you think about the problem, you've got a numerator and a denominator. The numerator is how good the system gets, and the denominator is how much energy goes into it. You believe you're seeing glimmers of the sign where both improve at the same time using this type of approach. Is that correct? Yes. When can we expect to hear more from you? Very soon. Amazing. Sovereignty: Out-of-Distribution

Palantir(163.0K)·34:40·10.9K views·2026-10-12
Alex KarpCEO, Palantir Louis RobsonLead, Palantir Sovereign
TL;DR"As soon as information ceases to be out-of-distribution, it ceases to be valuable, right?"
Palantir CEO Alex Karp and Palantir Sovereign lead Louis Robson discuss technological sovereignty, explaining how enterprises derive true value by protecting and ontologizing their unique "out-of-distribution" data and human artistry rather than relying on generic,...

TL;DR: Palantir CEO Alex Karp and Palantir Sovereign lead Louis Robson discuss technological sovereignty, explaining how enterprises derive true value by protecting and ontologizing their unique "out-of-distribution" data and human artistry rather than relying on generic, commoditized models.

Introduction: Talent and Background at Palantir

  • Background of Louis Robson:
    • Grew up in London before attending Stanford University to study physics, leaning toward the intersection of math, startup execution, and rigorous technical writing.
  • The Palantir Hiring Philosophy:
    • Palantir explicitly rejects standardized, resume-padding, hill-climbing career tracks in favor of identifying uniquely shaped, exceptionally high-aptitude individuals with specific "musical" traits.

The Economics of Sovereignty and Out-of-Distribution Data

  • Definition of Out-of-Distribution Data:
    • Unlike standard internet data, books, or generic classification sets, out-of-distribution information represents the unique, tacit knowledge and manufacturing of information generated exclusively by institutions.
  • The Value of Specificity:
    • As soon as information or data becomes accessible to everyone via generic models, it ceases to be valuable; true enterprise value relies on asymmetric, protected out-of-distribution insights.
  • Enterprise Telemetry:
    • Companies often underestimate their own tradecraft, focusing only on what they consciously know they are doing while missing the unmeasured telemetry—such as real-time problem assessment and parameter shifting—where true out-of-distribution value resides.

Limitations of Language and the Rise of Phenomenology

  • The Limits of Pure Language Models:
    • Classical AI models built solely on probabilistic language struggle with accurate representations of complex, multi-variable environments (like a game of Risk, a supply chain, or a battlefield).
  • Ontologies as Phenomenological Structures:
    • Ontologies act as opinionated compressions of information that are closer to pre-Kantian objects and real-world interactions (objects, relationships, kinetic sequences of decisions) than pure language allows.
  • Micro-Ontology Orchestration:
    • By capturing underlying institutional phenomenology into ontologies and selectively orchestrating micro-ontologies, organizations can dramatically increase their sovereignty factor and disrupt commoditized industries.

Moral Constraints, Doctrine, and System Governance

  • Soft Alignment vs. Hard Constraints:
    • Technological systems require a careful balance between soft alignment tasks and deterministic, rule-based hard constraints (e.g., access control determining precisely who can execute specific tasks).
  • Domestic vs. Battlefield Paradoxes:
    • The trade-offs between civil liberties (such as Fourth Amendment protections of privacy) and predictive technology differ vastly depending on whether an institution faces a domestic context or a peer-to-peer adversary.
  • Human Artistry and Management:
    • Enterprise and military leadership cannot force brilliance or creativity through first-principles reasoning; instead, leaders must learn to recognize, protect, and run with human artistry and institutional intuition when it emerges.

Notable Quotes

"As soon as information ceases to be out-of-distribution, it ceases to be valuable, right?" — Louis Robson
"If anybody can do it, if any model can do it, then it almost, you know, stops being valuable, right? It's like, if that, if anybody can do it, why, why does it matter that I can also do it?" — Louis Robson
"The secret to managing is not managing it, right? It's to know when it... you feel like it's cropping up and wielding the head of it's coming. But you can't completely understand when and how to... I can't force it." — Alex Karp

The philosophical thing I find fascinating, which is probably not exactly germane, is in Germanic philosophy as opposed to, like, Wittgenstein and the Linguistic Turn. I see this as the rise of phenomenology meeting language.
